Looking at main.tex, I can see the loaded packages include acl, times, latexsym, amsmath, amssymb, graphicx, booktabs, multirow, xcolor, subcaption, fontenc, inputenc, and microtype, but enumitem is missing. I'll add the enumitem package to main.tex to support the nosep option.

The bibtex error is "Illegal, another \bibstyle command" - this means there's a duplicate `\bibliography` or `\bibliographystyle` command. This happens when the appendix also has a `\bibliographystyle` command. Let me check the appendix.

Actually, this is a common LaTeX error. The `\bibliographystyle` appears twice because it's in both main.tex and possibly the appendix. Let me check.

From main.tex:
```


\bibliography{references}
\bibliographystyle{acl_natbib}
\appendix
\input{sections/appendix}
```

The appendix likely also has `\bibliographystyle` defined, which is causing the duplicate bibstyle error on line 1375 of main.aux. Even though bibtex threw an error, it still generated the .bbl file, so I should just re-run pdflatex to resolve the cross-references.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Looking at the layout, it seems like the paper is using a two-column format (ACL format). So what I'm seeing is:
- Page 9: Discussion (section 5) - left column and right column
- Page 10: Left column has the end of Discussion + Conclusion (section 6), right column has Limitations

In two-column format, the Limitations section starts in the right column of page 10 while the Conclusion is in the left column. So the main body (through conclusion) ends on page 10 left column.

For ACL, the page count counts full pages. 

Let me reconsider the page boundaries here. The Limitations section appears on page 10 but isn't counted toward the main body length, so I need to figure out where the actual body ends relative to the page numbers I'm seeing. Adding the Results, Discussion, and Conclusion sections brings the main body to about 10 pages—2 pages over the limit. The Related Work and Method sections are the best candidates for trimming, either by condensing them or moving some details to the appendix. I should flag this to the Director with specific recommendations before moving forward with Step 5.

  "content": "[Director 指令] **D036: PPL 命名修正 — 影响 Method + Results + Abstract**\n\nAgent 代码调查 + Reviewer 逻辑审查确认：论文中\"training perplexity\"命名混淆是 Claim 2 的核心问题。\n\n**事实**：\n- `training_loss` = train-set CE (HuggingFace Trainer logs) → within-gen AUC=0.923\n- `perplexity` = held-out validation PPL (signals.py compute_perplexity) → within-gen AUC=0.534\n- training PPL = exp(training loss)，是单调变换，AUC 应 ≈0.923\n- 因此 **training PPL 不是 misleading**。AUC=0.534 的是 **evaluation PPL**。\n\n**修正要求**：\n\n1. **Method §3.4**：\n   - Output signals 段落正式引入 3 个信号：`train_loss`（train-set CE，每 50 step）、`eval_perplexity`（held-out validation PPL，每 50 step）、`token_entropy`\n   - 明确写出 train_loss 和 eval_perplexity 的数据源区别\n   - 删除或修正 \"training perplexity is a misleading signal\" 的表述\n\n2. **Fig 3**：\n   - \"Training Loss\" 改标为 \"Train Loss (train-set CE)\"\n   - \"Perplexity\" 改标为 \"Eval PPL (held-out)\"\n\n3. **Results §4.2 (Claim 2)**：\n   - 核心叙事修正：不再是 \"training perplexity fails\"，而是 **\"held-out evaluation PPL provides no within-gen early warning (AUC=0.534), while train-set loss is an effective but interpretively ambiguous detector (AUC=0.923)\"**\n   - 讨论 train loss 有效的原因：它检测的是训练数据分布变化（contaminated vs clean），不是模型表征质量变化。loss 下降 ≠ 模型变好——模型只是成功拟合了污染数据\n   - Practitioner takeaway 修正：不能依赖 held-out eval PPL 做 within-gen 早期检测；train loss 变化有信号但需配合 spectral 信号交叉验证\n\n4. **Abstract**：\n   - \"training perplexity provides no within-generation early warning\" → \"held-out evaluation perplexity provides no within-generation early warning (AUC=0.53)\"\n\n5. **Fig 4 heatmap**：信号名对齐——\"Perplexity\" 改为 \"Eval PPL\"，确保 within-gen AUC 列用 0.534（eval PPL）而非 train loss 的值\n\n这是 D036 决策，影响多个章节。优先级高于 Step 3。"

_source line 1445 · 2026-05-15T23:49:18.595Z_

[Director 指令] **D036: PPL 命名修正 — 影响 Method + Results + Abstract**

Agent 代码调查 + Reviewer 逻辑审查确认：论文中"training perplexity"命名混淆是 Claim 2 的核心问题。

**事实**：
- `training_loss` = train-set CE (HuggingFace Trainer logs) → within-gen AUC=0.923
- `perplexity` = held-out validation PPL (signals.py compute_perplexity) → within-gen AUC=0.534
- training PPL = exp(training loss)，是单调变换，AUC 应 ≈0.923
- 因此 **training PPL 不是 misleading**。AUC=0.534 的是 **evaluation PPL**。

**修正要求**：

1. **Method §3.4**：
   - Output signals 段落正式引入 3 个信号：`train_loss`（train-set CE，每 50 step）、`eval_perplexity`（held-out validation PPL，每 50 step）、`token_entropy`
   - 明确写出 train_loss 和 eval_perplexity 的数据源区别
   - 删除或修正 "training perplexity is a misleading signal" 的表述

2. **Fig 3**：
   - "Training Loss" 改标为 "Train Loss (train-set CE)"
   - "Perplexity" 改标为 "Eval PPL (held-out)"

3. **Results §4.2 (Claim 2)**：
   - 核心叙事修正：不再是 "training perplexity fails"，而是 **"held-out evaluation PPL provides no within-gen early warning (AUC=0.534), while train-set loss is an effective but interpretively ambiguous detector (AUC=0.923)"**
   - 讨论 train loss 有效的原因：它检测的是训练数据分布变化（contaminated vs clean），不是模型表征质量变化。loss 下降 ≠ 模型变好——模型只是成功拟合了污染数据
   - Practitioner takeaway 修正：不能依赖 held-out eval PPL 做 within-gen 早期检测；train loss 变化有信号但需配合 spectral 信号交叉验证

4. **Abstract**：
   - "training perplexity provides no within-generation early warning" → "held-out evaluation perplexity provides no within-generation early warning (AUC=0.53)"

5. **Fig 4 heatmap**：信号名对齐——"Perplexity" 改为 "Eval PPL"，确保 within-gen AUC 列用 0.534（eval PPL）而非 train loss 的值

这是 D036 决策，影响多个章节。优先级高于 Step 3。

```text
41	\subsection{Within-Generation Early Warning}
42	\label{sec:early_warning_results}
43	
44	Cross-generation tracking (Section~\ref{sec:correlation}) requires completing multiple generations to assess severity.
45	A more actionable capability is detecting collapse \emph{within} a single training run, enabling early stopping before degradation accumulates.
46	We evaluate this using the relative deviation $\Delta_s$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:deviation}) and AUROC for binary collapse detection at each checkpoint.
47	
48	\paragraph{Stable rank as early alarm.}
49	At 10\% training progress, stable rank achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ for distinguishing $\alpha = 1.0$ from $\alpha = 0$ (Figure~\ref{fig:roc_curves}; Table~\ref{tab:within_gen_roc}).
50	At step~50 (3\% of training), $\Delta_\mathrm{SR} = 34.4\%$---far exceeding the 5\% detection threshold defined in Section~\ref{sec:evaluation}.
51	Stable rank's within-generation sensitivity reflects the structural nature of spectral signals: contamination alters the covariance eigenspectrum before output-level metrics respond, because energy dispersal across representation dimensions (Eq.~\ref{eq:stable_rank}) is detectable even when the model's token-level predictions have not yet degraded.
52	
53	% TODO: Figure~\ref{fig:roc_curves} uses synthetic ROC curves generated from known AUC values. Replace with empirical ROC data from plan001 5-seed validation.
54	
55	\paragraph{Spectral and token entropy.}
56	Token entropy deviates 24.5\% at step~50 but achieves only $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$ at 10\% progress---near random chance, indicating that despite its large initial deviation, token entropy provides no reliable within-generation discrimination.
57	Spectral entropy achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.773$, providing moderate within-generation discrimination but still substantially below stable rank's 0.984.
58	
59	\paragraph{Evaluation perplexity fails within-generation detection.}
60	Evaluation perplexity achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ at 10\% training progress---indistinguishable from random.
61	This failure has a clear mechanistic explanation.
62	Training perplexity measures fit to the current (potentially contaminated) data mix and can \emph{decrease} under collapse because the model fits its own synthetic output.
63	Evaluation perplexity, computed on clean held-out data, does track degradation in language modeling quality---but this degradation accumulates \emph{across} generations through the iterative contamination loop (Eq.~\ref{eq:mixing}), not within a single generation's training run.
64	Within one generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\mathcal{D}_g$; it has not yet produced the synthetic data that will contaminate $\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$.
65	Consequently, evaluation perplexity does not diverge from baseline within a single run, even when collapse is underway.
66	
67	Spectral signals, by contrast, detect structural change---the dispersal of representational energy across dimensions---independent of whether the model's next-token predictions have degraded on held-out text.
68	This structural sensitivity explains stable rank's near-perfect within-generation discrimination at a checkpoint where perplexity-based detection is no better than chance.
69	
70	% TODO: Within-gen ROC results are from N=1 seed at 0.5B (C1). Pending plan001 5-seed validation at 1.5B to determine AUC stability.
```

```text
66	
67	\paragraph{Output signals.}
68	We distinguish two perplexity regimes.
69	\emph{Training perplexity}, computed on the current (potentially contaminated) training batch, measures fit to the data mix; it can \emph{decrease} even as the model collapses, since the model fits its own synthetic output well.
70	\emph{Evaluation perplexity}, computed on a held-out clean set, reliably tracks degradation in language modeling quality on real text:
71	\begin{equation}
72	    \mathrm{PPL}_\text{eval} = \exp\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}|} \ell(\mathbf{x}_i)\right),
73	    \label{eq:perplexity}
74	\end{equation}
75	where $\ell(\mathbf{x}_i)$ is the cross-entropy loss on validation sequence $\mathbf{x}_i$ and $N$ is the total token count.
76	This distinction is critical for cross-generation severity tracking (Section~\ref{sec:evaluation}): training perplexity misleads, while evaluation perplexity reliably reflects degradation.
77	However, we additionally monitor \emph{training loss} $\mathcal{L}_\text{train}$ (the per-batch cross-entropy on the current training corpus) as a within-generation signal: the ease with which the model fits contaminated data produces a distinctive loss trajectory even when cross-generation severity is not yet apparent.
78	
79	\emph{Token entropy} measures the mean per-position entropy of the next-token distribution:
80	\begin{equation}
81	    \mathrm{TE}(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{L-1}\sum_{t=1}^{L-1} H\!\left(P_\theta(\cdot \mid x_{\leq t})\right), \quad H(P) = -\sum_v P(v) \log P(v),
82	    \label{eq:token_entropy}
83	\end{equation}
84	averaged over 100 validation chunks.
85	Decreasing token entropy indicates the model is becoming more peaked in its predictions, a hallmark of mode collapse in generation.
86	
87	\paragraph{Gradient signal.}
```

1	import matplotlib
2	matplotlib.use('Agg')
3	import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
4	import numpy as np
5	import json
6	import os
7	
8	plt.rcParams.update({
9	    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',
10	    'font.size': 10,
11	    'axes.titlesize': 11,
12	    'axes.labelsize': 10,
13	    'xtick.labelsize': 9,
14	    'ytick.labelsize': 9,
15	    'legend.fontsize': 8,
16	    'figure.dpi': 300,
17	    'savefig.dpi': 300,
18	    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',
19	    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.05,
20	    'axes.spines.top': False,
21	    'axes.spines.right': False,
22	    'lines.linewidth': 2.0,
23	})
24	
25	SIGNAL_COLORS = {
26	    'stable_rank': '#E69F00',
27	    'training_loss': '#009E73',
28	    'spectral_entropy': '#56B4E9',
29	    'perplexity': '#D55E00',
30	}
31	
32	SIGNAL_LABELS = {
33	    'stable_rank': 'Stable Rank',
34	    'training_loss': 'Training Loss',
35	    'spectral_entropy': 'Spectral Entropy',
36	    'perplexity': 'Perplexity',
37	}
38	
39	script_dir = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))
40	with open(os.path.join(script_dir, 'roc_curves_data.json')) as f:
41	    roc_data = json.load(f)
42	
43	fig, (ax1, ax2) = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(6.75, 2.8))
44	
45	# === Left panel: ROC curves (staircase) ===
46	ax1.plot([0, 1], [0, 1], 'k--', lw=0.8, alpha=0.4, label=None)
47	
48	for sig in ['stable_rank', 'training_loss', 'spectral_entropy', 'perplexity']:
49	    curve = roc_data['roc_curves'][sig]
50	    fpr = np.array(curve['fpr'])
51	    tpr = np.array(curve['tpr'])
52	    auc_val = np.trapezoid(tpr, fpr)
53	    ax1.step(fpr, tpr, where='post', color=SIGNAL_COLORS[sig],
54	             label=f'{SIGNAL_LABELS[sig]} (AUC={auc_val:.3f})')
55	
56	ax1.set_xlabel('False Positive Rate')
57	ax1.set_ylabel('True Positive Rate')
58	ax1.set_title('(a) Within-gen ROC Curves', fontweight='medium')
59	ax1.set_xlim(-0.02, 1.02)
60	ax1.set_ylim(-0.02, 1.02)
61	ax1.set_aspect('equal')
62	ax1.legend(loc='lower right', frameon=True, framealpha=0.9, edgecolor='0.8')
63	
64	# === Right panel: AUC vs Training Progress ===
65	ax2.axhline(y=0.5, color='k', ls='--', lw=0.8, alpha=0.4)
66	
67	progress_points = np.array(roc_data['progress_thresholds'])
68	
69	for sig in ['stable_rank', 'training_loss', 'spectral_entropy', 'perplexity']:
70	    ax2.plot(progress_points, roc_data['progress_auc'][sig],
71	             color=SIGNAL_COLORS[sig], marker='o', markersize=4,
72	             label=SIGNAL_LABELS[sig])
73	
74	sr_10pct_auc = roc_data['progress_auc']['stable_rank'][0]
75	ax2.annotate(f'AUC={sr_10pct_auc:.2f}',
76	             xy=(10, sr_10pct_auc), xytext=(22, sr_10pct_auc),
77	             fontsize=7.5, color=SIGNAL_COLORS['stable_rank'],
78	             arrowprops=dict(arrowstyle='->', color=SIGNAL_COLORS['stable_rank'], lw=1.0),
79	             va='center')

      "content": "import matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\nimport json\nimport os\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',\n    'font.size': 10,\n    'axes.titlesize': 11,\n    'axes.labelsize': 10,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 9,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 9,\n    'legend.fontsize': 8,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.05,\n    'axes.spines.top': False,\n    'axes.spines.right': False,\n    'lines.linewidth': 2.0,\n})\n\nSIGNAL_COLORS = {\n    'stable_rank': '#E69F00',\n    'training_loss': '#009E73',\n    'spectral_entropy': '#56B4E9',\n    'perplexity': '#D55E00',\n}\n\nSIGNAL_LABELS = {\n    'stable_rank': 'Stable Rank',\n    'training_loss': 'Training Loss',\n    'spectral_entropy': 'Spectral Entropy',\n    'perplexity': 'Perplexity',\n}\n\nscript_dir = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))\nwith open(os.path.join(script_dir, 'roc_curves_data.json')) as f:\n    roc_data = json.load(f)\n\nfig, (ax1, ax2) = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(6.75, 2.8))\n\n# === Left panel: ROC curves (staircase) ===\nax1.plot([0, 1], [0, 1], 'k--', lw=0.8, alpha=0.4, label=None)\n\nfor sig in ['stable_rank', 'training_loss', 'spectral_entropy', 'perplexity']:\n    curve = roc_data['roc_curves'][sig]\n    fpr = np.array(curve['fpr'])\n    tpr = np.array(curve['tpr'])\n    auc_val = np.trapezoid(tpr, fpr)\n    ax1.step(fpr, tpr, where='post', color=SIGNAL_COLORS[sig],\n             label=f'{SIGNAL_LABELS[sig]} (AUC={auc_val:.3f})')\n\nax1.set_xlabel('False Positive Rate')\nax1.set_ylabel('True Positive Rate')\nax1.set_title('(a) Within-gen ROC Curves', fontweight='medium')\nax1.set_xlim(-0.02, 1.02)\nax1.set_ylim(-0.02, 1.02)\nax1.set_aspect('equal')\nax1.legend(loc='lower right', frameon=True, framealpha=0.9, edgecolor='0.8')\n\n# === Right panel: AUC vs Training Progress ===\nax2.axhline(y=0.5, color='k', ls='--', lw=0.8, alpha=0.4)\n\nprogress_points = np.array(roc_data['progress_thresholds'])\n\nfor sig in ['stable_rank', 'training_loss', 'spectral_entropy', 'perplexity']:\n    ax2.plot(progress_points, roc_data['progress_auc'][sig],\n             color=SIGNAL_COLORS[sig], marker='o', markersize=4,\n             label=SIGNAL_LABELS[sig])\n\nsr_10pct_auc = roc_data['progress_auc']['stable_rank'][0]\nax2.annotate(f'AUC={sr_10pct_auc:.2f}',\n             xy=(10, sr_10pct_auc), xytext=(22, sr_10pct_auc),\n             fontsize=7.5, color=SIGNAL_COLORS['stable_rank'],\n             arrowprops=dict(arrowstyle='->', color=SIGNAL_COLORS['stable_rank'], lw=1.0),\n             va='center')\n\nax2.set_xlabel('Training Progress (%)')\nax2.set_ylabel('AUC')\nax2.set_title('(b) AUC vs Training Progress', fontweight='medium')\nax2.set_xlim(0, 105)\nax2.set_ylim(0.15, 1.02)\nax2.set_xticks([10, 20, 50, 100])\nax2.legend(loc='lower right', frameon=True, framealpha=0.9, edgecolor='0.8')\n\nfig.text(0.99, 0.02, 'N=1 seed, 0.5B (C1); pending multi-seed validation',\n         fontsize=7, color='0.55', ha='right', va='bottom',\n         fontstyle='italic')\n\nplt.tight_layout(w_pad=2.0)\nfig.subplots_adjust(bottom=0.18)\n\noutdir = script_dir\nfig.savefig(os.path.join(outdir, 'fig3_roc_curves.pdf'))\nfig.savefig(os.path.join(outdir, 'fig3_roc_curves.png'))\nplt.close()\n\nprint('Done.   "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_4/projects/collapse_severity_monitor/paper/sections/method.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Output signals.}\nWe distinguish two perplexity regimes.\n\\emph{Training perplexity}, computed on the current (potentially contaminated) training batch, measures fit to the data mix; it can \\emph{decrease} even as the model collapses, since the model fits its own synthetic output well.\n\\emph{Evaluation perplexity}, computed on a held-out clean set, reliably tracks degradation in language modeling quality on real text:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathrm{PPL}_\\text{eval} = \\exp\\!\\left(\\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{|\\mathcal{V}|} \\ell(\\mathbf{x}_i)\\right),\n    \\label{eq:perplexity}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\ell(\\mathbf{x}_i)$ is the cross-entropy loss on validation sequence $\\mathbf{x}_i$ and $N$ is the total token count.\nThis distinction is critical for cross-generation severity tracking (Section~\\ref{sec:evaluation}): training perplexity misleads, while evaluation perplexity reliably reflects degradation.\nHowever, we additionally monitor \\emph{training loss} $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{train}$ (the per-batch cross-entropy on the current training corpus) as a within-generation signal: the ease with which the model fits contaminated data produces a distinctive loss trajectory even when cross-generation severity is not yet apparent.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Output signals.}\nWe monitor three output-level signals computed on different data splits.\n\n\\emph{Train loss} $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{train}$ is the per-batch cross-entropy on the current training corpus, logged every 50 optimizer steps by the HuggingFace Trainer.\nBecause contaminated data has lower entropy than real text, train loss \\emph{decreases} under collapse---the model fits synthetic output easily.\nThis makes train loss an effective within-generation detector of data distribution change, though a decrease in train loss does not imply improved model quality.\n\n\\emph{Evaluation perplexity} is computed on a held-out clean validation set:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathrm{PPL}_\\text{eval} = \\exp\\!\\left(\\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{|\\mathcal{V}|} \\ell(\\mathbf{x}_i)\\right),\n    \\label{eq:perplexity}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\ell(\\mathbf{x}_i)$ is the cross-entropy loss on validation sequence $\\mathbf{x}_i$ and $N$ is the total token count.\nEvaluation perplexity tracks degradation in language modeling quality on real text across generations, but its within-generation utility is limited (Section~\\ref{sec:early_warning_results}).\nNote that training perplexity $\\mathrm{PPL}_\\text{train} = \\exp(\\mathcal{L}_\\text{train})$ is a monotone transform of train loss; we report train loss directly since it is the quantity logged during training."
}

===\nax1.plot([0, 1], [0, 1], 'k--', lw=0.8, alpha=0.4, label=None)\n\nfor sig in ['stable_rank', 'training_loss', 'spectral_entropy', 'perplexity']:\n    curve = roc_data['roc_curves'][sig]\n    fpr = np.array(curve['fpr'])\n    tpr = np.array(curve['tpr'])\n    auc_val = np.trapezoid(tpr, fpr)\n    ax1.step(fpr, tpr, where='post', color=SIGNAL_COLORS[sig],\n             label=f'{SIGNAL_LABELS[sig]} (AUC={auc_val:.3f})')\n\nax1.set_xlabel('False Positive Rate')\nax1.set_ylabel('True Positive Rate')\nax1.set_title('(a) Within-gen ROC Curves', {
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_4/projects/collapse_severity_monitor/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Evaluation perplexity fails within-generation detection.}\nEvaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ at 10\\% training progress---indistinguishable from random.\nThis failure has a clear mechanistic explanation.\nTraining perplexity measures fit to the current (potentially contaminated) data mix and can \\emph{decrease} under collapse because the model fits its own synthetic output.\nEvaluation perplexity, computed on clean held-out data, does track degradation in language modeling quality---but this degradation accumulates \\emph{across} generations through the iterative contamination loop (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mixing}), not within a single generation's training run.\nWithin one generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\\mathcal{D}_g$; it has not yet produced the synthetic data that will contaminate $\\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$.\nConsequently, evaluation perplexity does not diverge from baseline within a single run, even when collapse is underway.\n\nSpectral signals, by contrast, detect structural change---the dispersal of representational energy across dimensions---independent of whether the model's next-token predictions have degraded on held-out text.\nThis structural sensitivity explains stable rank's near-perfect within-generation discrimination at a checkpoint where perplexity-based detection is no better than chance.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Train loss: effective but interpretively ambiguous.}\nTrain loss achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$, the second-strongest within-generation signal after stable rank.\nIts effectiveness has a straightforward explanation: contaminated training data has lower entropy than real text, so the model achieves lower loss when the training corpus contains synthetic data.\nHowever, a decrease in train loss does not indicate improved model quality---it reflects successful fitting of corrupted data.\nPractitioners should therefore cross-validate train loss signals with spectral indicators to distinguish benign from pathological loss reduction.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation perplexity: no within-generation signal.}\nHeld-out evaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ at 10\\% training progress---indistinguishable from random.\nThis failure has a structural explanation: within a single generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\\mathcal{D}_g$ and has not yet produced the synthetic data that will contaminate $\\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mixing}).\nEvaluation perplexity accumulates degradation \\emph{across} generations through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single run.\nConsequently, it does not diverge from baseline within one generation, even when collapse is underway.\n\nSpectral signals, by contrast, detect structural change---the dispersal of representational energy across dimensions---independent of whether the model's held-out predictions have degraded.\nThis structural sensitivity explains stable rank's near-perfect within-generation discrimination at a checkpoint where evaluation perplexity is no better than chance."
}

### Tool result

_source line 1481 · 2026-05-15T23:50:29.041Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018u2bRNavr3N5uHQSJyrcKV`</summary>

```text
1	% Abstract — collapse_severity_monitor
2	% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B
3	
4	\begin{abstract}
5	Iterative training on model-generated data causes model collapse---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge---yet the metrics practitioners most commonly watch during training fail to detect it.
6	We systematically evaluate four families of training-time signals---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (evaluation perplexity, token entropy, training loss), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---as collapse severity monitors across five experimental configurations spanning model scales (0.5B--3B), fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), contamination fractions ($\alpha \in \{0, 0.1, \ldots, 1.0\}$), and text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed).
7	We find that different signal families have complementary operating ranges.
8	Spectral entropy achieves the largest cross-generation effect size (Cohen's $d = 33.48$, $\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), while stable rank provides the strongest within-generation early warning ($\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ at 10\% training progress, 34.4\% deviation at 3\% of training).
9	We document two negative results: evaluation perplexity achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation detection---indistinguishable from random---and token entropy achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$.
10	The same signal can be informative under one training method and misleading under another: gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.
11	These findings yield a three-tier monitoring strategy---stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, spectral entropy for severity discrimination---and a signal taxonomy characterizing where each family succeeds, saturates, or misleads.
12	\end{abstract}
13	
```

</details>

    "oldString": "We systematically evaluate four families of training-time signals---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (evaluation perplexity, token entropy, training loss), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---as collapse severity monitors across five experimental configurations spanning model scales (0.5B--3B), fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, \\ldots, 1.0\\}$), and text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed).",
    "newString": "We systematically evaluate four families of training-time signals---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---as collapse severity monitors across five experimental configurations spanning model scales (0.5B--3B), fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, \\ldots, 1.0\\}$), and text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed).",
    "originalFile": "% Abstract — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nIterative training on model-generated data causes model collapse---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge---yet the metrics practitioners most commonly watch during training fail to detect it.\nWe systematically evaluate four families of training-time signals---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (evaluation perplexity, token entropy, training loss), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---as collapse severity monitors across five experimental configurations spanning model scales (0.5B--3B), fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, \\ldots, 1.0\\}$), and text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed).\nWe find that different signal families have complementary operating ranges.\nSpectral entropy achieves the largest cross-generation effect size (Cohen's $d = 33.48$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), while stable rank provides the strongest within-generation early warning ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ at 10\\% training progress, 34.4\\% deviation at 3\\% of training).\nWe document two negative results: evaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation detection---indistinguishable from random---and token entropy achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$.\nThe same signal can be informative under one training method and misleading under another: gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.\nThese findings yield a three-tier monitoring strategy---stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, spectral entropy for severity discrimination---and a signal taxonomy characterizing where each family succeeds, saturates, or misleads.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

          "+We systematically evaluate four families of training-time signals---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---as collapse severity monitors across five experimental configurations spanning model scales (0.5B--3B), fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, \\ldots, 1.0\\}$), and text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed).",

```text
28	\subsection{Evaluation Perplexity: Within-Generation Failure Mechanism}
29	\label{sec:ppl_failure}
30	
31	Evaluation perplexity achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ at 10\% training---indistinguishable from chance---despite being the primary severity indicator across generations.
32	This failure has a structural explanation.
33	Within a single generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\mathcal{D}_g$; collapse pressure enters only at the next generation when synthetic output from $\tilde{M}_g$ contaminates $\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:mixing}).
34	Evaluation perplexity accumulates degradation through the iterative loop, not within a single run.
35	Training perplexity is worse still: it measures fit to the current (potentially contaminated) data and can \emph{decrease} as the model fits its own synthetic output.
36	Spectral signals, by contrast, detect structural change---the dispersal of representational energy across dimensions---independent of data quality.
37	For practitioners, the implication is direct: perplexity is a lagging indicator of collapse, and relying on it alone risks missing the first generation of degradation entirely.
38	
39	\subsection{Partial Recovery at Moderate Contamination}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019no6DM6t5Zrupbh2v18djq`</summary>

```text
8	This recursive dynamic creates self-consuming loops that cause \emph{model collapse}---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge~\citep{Shumailov2024,Alemohammad2024}.
9	The problem compounds: even trace synthetic contamination induces collapse at sufficient depth~\citep{Dohmatob2025}, with errors accumulating across iterations at accelerating rates~\citep{Dohmatob2024_tails,Fu2024_icml}.
10	Yet the metrics practitioners most commonly watch during training fail to detect this degradation within a run.
11	Training perplexity can \emph{decrease} as a model collapses, since it measures fit to data that is itself partially synthetic.
12	Evaluation perplexity, computed on clean held-out data, reliably tracks severity \emph{across} generations but achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation collapse detection---indistinguishable from random chance.
13	A practitioner watching only perplexity would have no warning that collapse is underway until the next generation of synthetic data has already been produced.
14	
15	Existing work addresses model collapse from two directions.
```

    "originalFile": "% Discussion section — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B, heatmap_data.json\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Practical Three-Tier Monitoring Recommendation}\n\\label{sec:three_tier}\n\nNo single signal suffices for collapse monitoring across all operating regimes.\nOur results motivate a three-tier strategy that assigns each signal family to the task where it excels:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep]\n    \\item \\textbf{Within-generation alarm} $\\to$ stable rank.\n    At 10\\% training progress, stable rank achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ for binary collapse detection, with $\\Delta_\\mathrm{SR} = 34.4\\%$ at step~50 (3\\% of training).\n    This structural sensitivity---detecting energy dispersal across representation dimensions before output-level metrics respond---provides the earliest actionable warning.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-generation severity tracking} $\\to$ evaluation perplexity.\n    With a large absolute effect size ($d = 20.82$) and monotonic growth across generations, evaluation perplexity provides an interpretable severity scale that maps directly to language modeling quality on held-out text.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Severity discrimination} $\\to$ spectral entropy.\n    The largest effect size ($d = 33.48$) and lowest coefficient of variation ($\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$) across seeds make spectral entropy the most reproducible discriminator between contamination levels.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\noindent\nThis assignment reflects a core finding: the signal that best detects collapse onset (stable rank, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$, seed-sensitive) differs from the one that most reproducibly quantifies its severity (spectral entropy).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Perplexity: Within-Generation Failure Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:ppl_failure}\n\nEvaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ at 10\\% training---indistinguishable from chance---despite being the primary severity indicator across generations.\nThis failure has a structural explanation.\nWithin a single generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\\mathcal{D}_g$; collapse pressure enters only at the next generation when synthetic output from $\\tilde{M}_g$ contaminates $\\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mixing}).\nEvaluation perplexity accumulates degradation through the iterative loop, not within a single run.\nTraining perplexity is worse still: it measures fit to the current (potentially contaminated) data and can \\emph{decrease} as the model fits its own synthetic output.\nSpectral signals, by contrast, detect structural change---the dispersal of representational energy across dimensions---independent of data quality.\nFor practitioners, the implication is direct: perplexity is a lagging indicator of collapse, and relying on it alone risks missing the first generation of degradation entirely.\n\n\\subsection{Partial Recovery at Moderate Contamination}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nAt $\\alpha = 0.5$, some signals show partial recovery across generations: output signals recover approximately 52\\% of the clean-to-collapsed gap, while weight-space signals recover approximately 33\\%.\nThis pattern is consistent with the data accumulation effects documented by \\citet{Gerstgrasser2024}, where mixing real data with synthetic data attenuates collapse.\nWe note this as an observation, not a novel finding.\nThe practical consequence is that monitoring thresholds should be calibrated to the expected contamination level rather than set as binary collapsed/not-collapsed decisions.\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Concurrent Monitoring Approaches}\n\\label{sec:sigma_comparison}\n\nThe signals evaluated here are zero-cost: they are computed during standard training, require no clean reference distribution, and provide per-layer granularity.\nSIGMA~\\citep{Gu2026} operates post-hoc on the output embedding Gram matrix and requires a clean reference distribution for comparison.\n\\citet{Kalinowski2026} uses persistent homology, incurring higher computational cost from a different signal family entirely.\nNeither provides the systematic comparison of signal families, operating range characterization across contamination fractions, or within-generation early warning documented here.\nThese approaches are complementary: SIGMA and topological methods may capture aspects of collapse that spectral covariance signals miss, while our signals offer the advantage of online, reference-free monitoring integrated into the training loop.\n\n\\subsection{Terminal Spectral Convergence}\n\\label{sec:terminal_convergence}\n\nAt generation~4, $\\alpha = 1.0$, terminal stable rank values are $\\mathrm{SR} = 153.87$ (full fine-tuning) versus $155.64$ (QLoRA)---a 1.2\\% difference despite markedly different collapse dynamics (onset ratio $15.3\\times$ vs $8.7\\times$; terminal $\\mathrm{PPL}_\\text{eval}$ of $326.19$ vs $839.2$).\nThis observation rests on $N = 1$ seed per method and cannot be distinguished from coincidence.\nIf real, it would suggest that iterative synthetic contamination drives representations toward a method-independent attractor in spectral space---a convergence in representational structure despite divergent output-level severity.\nMulti-seed validation is required before this can be treated as anything more than speculation.\n",

    "oldString": "Yet the metrics practitioners most commonly watch during training fail to detect this degradation within a run.\nTraining perplexity can \\emph{decrease} as a model collapses, since it measures fit to data that is itself partially synthetic.\nEvaluation perplexity, computed on clean held-out data, reliably tracks severity \\emph{across} generations but achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation collapse detection---indistinguishable from random chance.\nA practitioner watching only perplexity would have no warning that collapse is underway until the next generation of synthetic data has already been produced.",
    "newString": "Yet the metric practitioners most commonly rely on for quality assessment---held-out evaluation perplexity---fails to detect this degradation within a run, achieving $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation collapse detection (indistinguishable from random chance).\nTrain loss does change under collapse ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), but its decrease is interpretively ambiguous: the model fits lower-entropy synthetic data more easily, which looks like healthy convergence.\nA practitioner watching only perplexity or interpreting train loss at face value would have no reliable warning that collapse is underway.",
    "originalFile": "% Introduction — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nAs LLM-generated text pervades web-scraped training corpora, practitioners increasingly fine-tune models on data shaped by prior model generations.\nThis recursive dynamic creates self-consuming loops that cause \\emph{model collapse}---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge~\\citep{Shumailov2024,Alemohammad2024}.\nThe problem compounds: even trace synthetic contamination induces collapse at sufficient depth~\\citep{Dohmatob2025}, with errors accumulating across iterations at accelerating rates~\\citep{Dohmatob2024_tails,Fu2024_icml}.\nYet the metrics practitioners most commonly watch during training fail to detect this degradation within a run.\nTraining perplexity can \\emph{decrease} as a model collapses, since it measures fit to data that is itself partially synthetic.\nEvaluation perplexity, computed on clean held-out data, reliably tracks severity \\emph{across} generations but achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation collapse detection---indistinguishable from random chance.\nA practitioner watching only perplexity would have no warning that collapse is underway until the next generation of synthetic data has already been produced.\n\nExisting work addresses model collapse from two directions.\nOn the theoretical side, \\citet{Shumailov2024} established the phenomenon, \\citet{Dohmatob2025} proved that any nonzero synthetic fraction eventually induces strong collapse, and \\citet{Dohmatob2024_tails} showed that collapse alters neural scaling laws.\nOn the mitigation side, data accumulation~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}, loss modification~\\citep{Zibakhsh2025}, and verification-based filtering~\\citep{Feng2025} each reduce collapse pressure under specific conditions.\nMissing from this landscape is a systematic analysis of which \\emph{training-time signals} best track collapse severity, when they provide early warning, and where they saturate or actively mislead.\n\\citet{Schaeffer2025} highlight that even the definition of collapse varies across studies, further motivating signal-level analysis grounded in controlled experiments.\nThe two closest concurrent works---SIGMA~\\citep{Gu2026}, which monitors output embedding spectra, and the topological index of \\citet{Kalinowski2026}---each address part of this gap but carry limitations: SIGMA requires a clean reference distribution and operates post-hoc; topological monitoring incurs higher computational cost.\nNeither provides a cross-family comparison of signal operating ranges or quantitative within-generation early warning thresholds.\n\nWe conduct a systematic empirical study of training-time signals as collapse severity monitors.\nWe evaluate four signal families---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (evaluation perplexity, token entropy, training loss), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---across five experimental configurations spanning model scales (0.5B to 3B), fine-tuning methods (QLoRA and full fine-tuning), contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\\}$), and text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed).\nOur central finding is that different signal families have complementary operating ranges, and the same signal's utility can flip from informative to misleading depending on the training method.\n\nOur contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal--severity correlation.}\n    Spectral and output signals track collapse across contamination levels.\n    Spectral entropy achieves the largest effect size (Cohen's $d = 33.48$, coefficient of variation $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), followed by evaluation perplexity ($d = 20.82$) and stable rank ($d = 9.43$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$).\n    Validated across QLoRA and full fine-tuning, six of seven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change regardless of training method.\n    The exception---gradient norm, which is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning---demonstrates that method-dependence is a first-order concern for monitoring pipeline design.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Within-generation early warning and negative results.}\n    Stable rank achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ at 10\\% training progress for binary collapse detection, with 34.4\\% deviation from baseline at 3\\% of training.\n    Training loss provides the second-strongest within-generation signal ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$).\n    We document two negative results: evaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation detection---the most commonly available metric fails at the most actionable task---and token entropy achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$, providing no reliable discrimination despite a large initial deviation.\n    These failures have a structural explanation: evaluation perplexity accumulates degradation through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single generation's training, while token entropy's per-position measurement lacks the representational sensitivity of spectral signals.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal taxonomy with method-dependent operating ranges.}\n    Signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method and model scale.\n    Gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.\n    HellaSwag accuracy is insensitive at 0.5B scale ($d < 1$ under QLoRA).\n    Distinct-1 is non-monotonic, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.\n    Full fine-tuning produces sharper collapse onset ($15.3\\times$ evaluation perplexity ratio at generation~1) than QLoRA ($8.7\\times$) but lower terminal severity.\n    These findings motivate a three-tier recommendation: stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, and spectral entropy for severity discrimination.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys collapse theory, spectral analysis of neural networks, and concurrent monitoring approaches.\nSection~\\ref{sec:method} details our iterative contamination protocol, signal definitions, and evaluation framework.\nSection~\\ref{sec:results} presents signal--severity correlations, within-generation early warning analysis, and the method-dependent signal taxonomy.\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses practical monitoring recommendations and limitations.\nSection~\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes.\n",

plan003-fullft-05B\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe presented a systematic empirical study of training-time signals as model collapse severity monitors.\nAcross five experimental configurations spanning two model scales (0.5B--3B), two text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed), two fine-tuning methods (QLoRA, full fine-tuning), and six contamination fractions, we established three findings.\n\nFirst, spectral and output signals reliably track collapse severity across configurations.\nSpectral entropy achieves the largest and most reproducible effect size ($d = 33.48$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), followed by evaluation perplexity ($d = 20.82$) and stable rank ($d = 9.43$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$).\nSix of seven monitored signals maintain consistent task.\n\nThird, signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method and model scale, motivating a three-tier monitoring strategy: stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, and spectral entropy for severity discrimination.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nValidating these findings at scales of practical interest ($\\geq$7B) and across diverse domains would establish generalizability.\nTranslating early warning signals into automated intervention triggers---adaptive learning rate reduction, selective data filtering, or training rollback when spectral deviation exceeds calibrated thresholds---would close the loop from detection to mitigation.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KhWAaLhdXvCBQNQFSZZdqF`</summary>

```text
35	    \item \textbf{Within-generation early warning and negative results.}
36	    Stable rank achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ at 10\% training progress for binary collapse detection, with 34.4\% deviation from baseline at 3\% of training.
37	    Training loss provides the second-strongest within-generation signal ($\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$).
38	    We document two negative results: evaluation perplexity achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation detection---the most commonly available metric fails at the most actionable task---and token entropy achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$, providing no reliable discrimination despite a large initial deviation.
39	    These failures have a structural explanation: evaluation perplexity accumulates degradation through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single generation's training, while token entropy's per-position measurement lacks the representational sensitivity of spectral signals.
40	
41	    \item \textbf{Signal taxonomy with method-dependent operating ranges.}
42	    Signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method and model scale.
43	    Gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.
44	    HellaSwag accuracy is insensitive at 0.5B scale ($d < 1$ under QLoRA).
45	    Distinct-1 is non-monotonic, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.
46	    Full fine-tuning produces sharper collapse onset ($15.3\times$ evaluation perplexity ratio at generation~1) than QLoRA ($8.7\times$) but lower terminal severity.
```

    "oldString": "    Training loss provides the second-strongest within-generation signal ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$).\n    We document two negative results: evaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation detection---the most commonly available metric fails at the most actionable task---and token entropy achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$, providing no reliable discrimination despite a large initial deviation.\n    These failures have a structural explanation: evaluation perplexity accumulates degradation through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single generation's training, while token entropy's per-position measurement lacks the representational sensitivity of spectral signals.",
    "newString": "    Train loss provides the second-strongest within-generation signal ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), though its decrease reflects successful fitting of corrupted data rather than improved model quality.\n    Held-out evaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation detection---the most commonly relied-upon quality metric fails at the most actionable task---and token entropy achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$, providing no reliable discrimination despite a large initial deviation.\n    Evaluation perplexity's failure has a structural explanation: it accumulates degradation through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single generation's training.",
    "originalFile": "% Introduction — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nAs LLM-generated text pervades web-scraped training corpora, practitioners increasingly fine-tune models on data shaped by prior model generations.\nThis recursive dynamic creates self-consuming loops that cause \\emph{model collapse}---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge~\\citep{Shumailov2024,Alemohammad2024}.\nThe problem compounds: even trace synthetic contamination induces collapse at sufficient depth~\\citep{Dohmatob2025}, with errors accumulating across iterations at accelerating rates~\\citep{Dohmatob2024_tails,Fu2024_icml}.\nYet the metric practitioners most commonly rely on for quality assessment---held-out evaluation perplexity---fails to detect this degradation within a run, achieving $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation collapse detection (indistinguishable from random chance).\nTrain loss does change under collapse ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), but its decrease is interpretively ambiguous: the model fits lower-entropy synthetic data more easily, which looks like healthy convergence.\nA practitioner watching only perplexity or interpreting train loss at face value would have no reliable warning that collapse is underway.\n\nExisting work addresses model collapse from two directions.\nOn the theoretical side, \\citet{Shumailov2024} established the phenomenon, \\citet{Dohmatob2025} proved that any nonzero synthetic fraction eventually induces strong collapse, and \\citet{Dohmatob2024_tails} showed that collapse alters neural scaling laws.\nOn the mitigation side, data accumulation~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}, loss modification~\\citep{Zibakhsh2025}, and verification-based filtering~\\citep{Feng2025} each reduce collapse pressure under specific conditions.\nMissing from this landscape is a systematic analysis of which \\emph{training-time signals} best track collapse severity, when they provide early warning, and where they saturate or actively mislead.\n\\citet{Schaeffer2025} highlight that even the definition of collapse varies across studies, further motivating signal-level analysis grounded in controlled experiments.\nThe two closest concurrent works---SIGMA~\\citep{Gu2026}, which monitors output embedding spectra, and the topological index of \\citet{Kalinowski2026}---each address part of this gap but carry limitations: SIGMA requires a clean reference distribution and operates post-hoc; topological monitoring incurs higher computational cost.\nNeither provides a cross-family comparison of signal operating ranges or quantitative within-generation early warning thresholds.\n\nWe conduct a systematic empirical study of training-time signals as collapse severity monitors.\nWe evaluate four signal families---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (evaluation perplexity, token entropy, training loss), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---across five experimental configurations spanning model scales (0.5B to 3B), fine-tuning methods (QLoRA and full fine-tuning), contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\\}$), and text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed).\nOur central finding is that different signal families have complementary operating ranges, and the same signal's utility can flip from informative to misleading depending on the training method.\n\nOur contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal--severity correlation.}\n    Spectral and output signals track collapse across contamination levels.\n    Spectral entropy achieves the largest effect size (Cohen's $d = 33.48$, coefficient of variation $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), followed by evaluation perplexity ($d = 20.82$) and stable rank ($d = 9.43$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$).\n    Validated across QLoRA and full fine-tuning, six of seven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change regardless of training method.\n    The exception---gradient norm, which is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning---demonstrates that method-dependence is a first-order concern for monitoring pipeline design.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Within-generation early warning and negative results.}\n    Stable rank achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ at 10\\% training progress for binary collapse detection, with 34.4\\% deviation from baseline at 3\\% of training.\n    Training loss provides the second-strongest within-generation signal ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$).\n    We document two negative results: evaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation detection---the most commonly available metric fails at the most actionable task---and token entropy achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$, providing no reliable discrimination despite a large initial deviation.\n    These failures have a structural explanation: evaluation perplexity accumulates degradation through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single generation's training, while token entropy's per-position measurement lacks the representational sensitivity of spectral signals.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal taxonomy with method-dependent operating ranges.}\n    Signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method and model scale.\n    Gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.\n    HellaSwag accuracy is insensitive at 0.5B scale ($d < 1$ under QLoRA).\n    Distinct-1 is non-monotonic, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.\n    Full fine-tuning produces sharper collapse onset ($15.3\\times$ evaluation perplexity ratio at generation~1) than QLoRA ($8.7\\times$) but lower terminal severity.\n    These findings motivate a three-tier recommendation: stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, and spectral entropy for severity discrimination.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys collapse theory, spectral analysis of neural networks, and concurrent monitoring approaches.\nSection~\\ref{sec:method} details our iterative contamination protocol, signal definitions, and evaluation framework.\nSection~\\ref{sec:results} presents signal--severity correlations, within-generation early warning analysis, and the method-dependent signal taxonomy.\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses practical monitoring recommendations and limitations.\nSection~\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes.\n",

[Director 指令] **Training Loss vs Perplexity 调查完毕（D035）— Method + Fig 3 修正指令**

Agent 查明代码定义：
- **training_loss** = Trainer running avg CE on **训练集**（含 synthetic 混合），`train.py:109-110`。Within-gen AUC=0.923。
- **perplexity** = exp(NLL) on **固定 held-out 验证集**，`signals.py:92-108`。Within-gen AUC=0.534。

两者数据源不同，不是单调变换，AUC 差异合理。paper_framing.md 已更新（Note 19 重写）。

**Method 修正**：
1. 在 §3.3 Output signals 中加入 **training_loss** 作为正式信号，明确标注：train-set CE (Trainer logged, every 50 steps)
2. 已有的 **perplexity** 必须明确标注：held-out validation PPL (model.eval(), every 50 steps)
3. 信号总数从 6 → 7（加 training_loss）

**Fig 3 信号集**：展示 training_loss 是合理的（within-gen 第二强），与更新后的 Method 一致。

**Results §4.2 叙事**：
- Featured sub-result 修正为："held-out evaluation perplexity — the metric practitioners most commonly check at eval checkpoints — provides no within-generation early warning (AUC=0.534)"
- 新增 1-2 句解释 training_loss 为什么有效：它直接测量模型对被污染训练分布的学习动态
- 三层对比：stable_rank (AUC=0.984, 权重结构) > training_loss (AUC=0.923, 训练数据分布) > held-out PPL (AUC=0.534, 泛化)

**Abstract/Intro**：从 "training perplexity" 改为 "held-out evaluation perplexity"。

这个修正实际上**强化**了论文分析深度——从"PPL 不行"升级为"不同数据源的信号有不同检测能力，held-out metrics 是 within-gen 最弱环节"。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JkWoy1BEnJMCeNF4Rftt3Z`</summary>

```text
59	\paragraph{Train loss: effective but interpretively ambiguous.}
60	Train loss achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$, the second-strongest within-generation signal after stable rank.
61	Its effectiveness has a straightforward explanation: contaminated training data has lower entropy than real text, so the model achieves lower loss when the training corpus contains synthetic data.
62	However, a decrease in train loss does not indicate improved model quality---it reflects successful fitting of corrupted data.
63	Practitioners should therefore cross-validate train loss signals with spectral indicators to distinguish benign from pathological loss reduction.
64	
65	\paragraph{Evaluation perplexity: no within-generation signal.}
66	Held-out evaluation perplexity achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ at 10\% training progress---indistinguishable from random.
67	This failure has a structural explanation: within a single generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\mathcal{D}_g$ and has not yet produced the synthetic data that will contaminate $\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:mixing}).
68	Evaluation perplexity accumulates degradation \emph{across} generations through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single run.
69	Consequently, it does not diverge from baseline within one generation, even when collapse is underway.
70	
71	Spectral signals, by contrast, detect structural change---the dispersal of representational energy across dimensions---independent of whether the model's held-out predictions have degraded.
72	This structural sensitivity explains stable rank's near-perfect within-generation discrimination at a checkpoint where evaluation perplexity is no better than chance.
73	
74	% TODO: Within-gen ROC results are from N=1 seed at 0.5B (C1). Pending plan001 5-seed validation at 1.5B to determine AUC stability.
75	
76	
77	\subsection{Signal Taxonomy: Method-Dependent Operating Ranges}
78	\label{sec:taxonomy}
```

      "content": "\\paragraph{Train loss: effective but interpretively ambiguous.}\nTrain loss achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$, the second-strongest within-generation signal after stable rank.\nIts effectiveness has a straightforward explanation: contaminated training data has lower entropy than real text, so the model achieves lower loss when the training corpus contains synthetic data.\nHowever, a decrease in train loss does not indicate improved model quality---it reflects successful fitting of corrupted data.\nPractitioners should therefore cross-validate train loss signals with spectral indicators to distinguish benign from pathological loss reduction.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation perplexity: no within-generation signal.}\nHeld-out evaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ at 10\\% training progress---indistinguishable from random.\nThis failure has a structural explanation: within a single generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\\mathcal{D}_g$ and has not yet produced the synthetic data that will contaminate $\\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mixing}).\nEvaluation perplexity accumulates degradation \\emph{across} generations through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single run.\nConsequently, it does not diverge from baseline within one generation, even when collapse is underway.\n\nSpectral signals, by contrast, detect structural change---the dispersal of representational energy across dimensions---independent of whether the model's held-out predictions have degraded.\nThis structural sensitivity explains stable rank's near-perfect within-generation discrimination at a checkpoint where evaluation perplexity is no better than chance.\n\n% TODO: Within-gen ROC results are from N=1 seed at 0.5B (C1). Pending plan001 5-seed validation at 1.5B to determine AUC stability.\n\n\n\\subsection{Signal Taxonomy: Method-Dependent Operating Ranges}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy}",

    "newString": "We evaluate four signal families---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (train loss, evaluation perplexity, token entropy), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---across five experimental configurations spanning model scales (0.5B to 3B), fine-tuning methods (QLoRA and full fine-tuning), contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\\}$), and text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed).",
    "originalFile": "% Introduction — collapse_severity_monitor\n% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nAs LLM-generated text pervades web-scraped training corpora, practitioners increasingly fine-tune models on data shaped by prior model generations.\nThis recursive dynamic creates self-consuming loops that cause \\emph{model collapse}---progressive loss of distribution tails, contraction of output diversity, and degradation of acquired knowledge~\\citep{Shumailov2024,Alemohammad2024}.\nThe problem compounds: even trace synthetic contamination induces collapse at sufficient depth~\\citep{Dohmatob2025}, with errors accumulating across iterations at accelerating rates~\\citep{Dohmatob2024_tails,Fu2024_icml}.\nYet the metric practitioners most commonly rely on for quality assessment---held-out evaluation perplexity---fails to detect this degradation within a run, achieving $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation collapse detection (indistinguishable from random chance).\nTrain loss does change under collapse ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), but its decrease is interpretively ambiguous: the model fits lower-entropy synthetic data more easily, which looks like healthy convergence.\nA practitioner watching only perplexity or interpreting train loss at face value would have no reliable warning that collapse is underway.\n\nExisting work addresses model collapse from two directions.\nOn the theoretical side, \\citet{Shumailov2024} established the phenomenon, \\citet{Dohmatob2025} proved that any nonzero synthetic fraction eventually induces strong collapse, and \\citet{Dohmatob2024_tails} showed that collapse alters neural scaling laws.\nOn the mitigation side, data accumulation~\\citep{Gerstgrasser2024}, loss modification~\\citep{Zibakhsh2025}, and verification-based filtering~\\citep{Feng2025} each reduce collapse pressure under specific conditions.\nMissing from this landscape is a systematic analysis of which \\emph{training-time signals} best track collapse severity, when they provide early warning, and where they saturate or actively mislead.\n\\citet{Schaeffer2025} highlight that even the definition of collapse varies across studies, further motivating signal-level analysis grounded in controlled experiments.\nThe two closest concurrent works---SIGMA~\\citep{Gu2026}, which monitors output embedding spectra, and the topological index of \\citet{Kalinowski2026}---each address part of this gap but carry limitations: SIGMA requires a clean reference distribution and operates post-hoc; topological monitoring incurs higher computational cost.\nNeither provides a cross-family comparison of signal operating ranges or quantitative within-generation early warning thresholds.\n\nWe conduct a systematic empirical study of training-time signals as collapse severity monitors.\nWe evaluate four signal families---spectral (stable rank, spectral entropy), output (evaluation perplexity, token entropy, training loss), gradient (L2 norm), and downstream (HellaSwag accuracy)---across five experimental configurations spanning model scales (0.5B to 3B), fine-tuning methods (QLoRA and full fine-tuning), contamination fractions ($\\alpha \\in \\{0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0\\}$), and text domains (WikiText-2, PubMed).\nOur central finding is that different signal families have complementary operating ranges, and the same signal's utility can flip from informative to misleading depending on the training method.\n\nOur contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal--severity correlation.}\n    Spectral and output signals track collapse across contamination levels.\n    Spectral entropy achieves the largest effect size (Cohen's $d = 33.48$, coefficient of variation $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$ across seeds), followed by evaluation perplexity ($d = 20.82$) and stable rank ($d = 9.43$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$).\n    Validated across QLoRA and full fine-tuning, six of seven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change regardless of training method.\n    The exception---gradient norm, which is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning---demonstrates that method-dependence is a first-order concern for monitoring pipeline design.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Within-generation early warning and negative results.}\n    Stable rank achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ at 10\\% training progress for binary collapse detection, with 34.4\\% deviation from baseline at 3\\% of training.\n    Train loss provides the second-strongest within-generation signal ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$), though its decrease reflects successful fitting of corrupted data rather than improved model quality.\n    Held-out evaluation perplexity achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ for within-generation detection---the most commonly relied-upon quality metric fails at the most actionable task---and token entropy achieves $\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$, providing no reliable discrimination despite a large initial deviation.\n    Evaluation perplexity's failure has a structural explanation: it accumulates degradation through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single generation's training.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Signal taxonomy with method-dependent operating ranges.}\n    Signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method and model scale.\n    Gradient norm is non-monotonic under QLoRA but monotonic under full fine-tuning.\n    HellaSwag accuracy is insensitive at 0.5B scale ($d < 1$ under QLoRA).\n    Distinct-1 is non-monotonic, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.\n    Full fine-tuning produces sharper collapse onset ($15.3\\times$ evaluation perplexity ratio at generation~1) than QLoRA ($8.7\\times$) but lower terminal severity.\n    These findings motivate a three-tier recommendation: stable rank for within-generation alarms, evaluation perplexity for cross-generation tracking, and spectral entropy for severity discrimination.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys collapse theory, spectral analysis of neural networks, and concurrent monitoring approaches.\nSection~\\ref{sec:method} details our iterative contamination protocol, signal definitions, and evaluation framework.\nSection~\\ref{sec:results} presents signal--severity correlations, within-generation early warning analysis, and the method-dependent signal taxonomy.\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses practical monitoring recommendations and limitations.\nSection~\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes.\n",
