<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LN5nXiBigiwCsB5Mxnq1nY`</summary>

```text
1	% Results section — collapse_severity_monitor
2	% Data sources: plan006 (3-seed Cohen's d), within_gen_analysis, plan003-fullft-05B, heatmap_data.json
3	
4	\section{Results}
5	\label{sec:results}
6	
7	We organize results around three claims.
8	Section~\ref{sec:correlation} establishes that spectral and output signals track collapse severity across configurations.
9	Section~\ref{sec:early_warning_results} shows that certain signals detect collapse within a single training run before evaluation perplexity diverges.
10	Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy} reveals that signal informativeness depends on the fine-tuning method, motivating a taxonomy of method-dependent operating ranges.
11	
12	\subsection{Signal--Severity Correlation}
13	\label{sec:correlation}
14	
15	We quantify signal sensitivity using Cohen's $d$ between the clean baseline ($\alpha = 0$) and full contamination ($\alpha = 1.0$) at generation~4, computed across three seeds (C1 configuration; Table~\ref{tab:configs}).
16	Effect sizes, rather than rank correlations, serve as the primary evidence because they convey the magnitude of signal separation, not just its ordering.
17	
18	\paragraph{Effect sizes.}
19	Spectral entropy achieves the largest and most reproducible effect size ($d = 33.48$, coefficient of variation across seeds $\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$), followed by evaluation perplexity ($d = 20.82$), distinct-1 ($d = 14.60$), and stable rank ($d = 9.43$, $\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$).
20	Distinct-2 ($d = 7.59$) and distinct-3 ($d = -7.08$; negative because collapse increases trigram repetition) provide moderate separation.
21	Token entropy, self-BLEU, gradient norm, and HellaSwag accuracy yield small effect sizes ($d < 5$), placing them in a second tier for cross-generation severity discrimination (Figure~\ref{fig:severity_curves}).
22	
23	Stable rank warrants a cautionary note on seed sensitivity.
24	With two seeds, $d = 61.7$; adding a third seed reduced this to $d = 9.43$---a rank change from 1st to 4th among monitored signals.
25	Its $\mathrm{CV}$ of 0.022 is an order of magnitude larger than spectral entropy's 0.0015.
26	We treat stable rank as informative but seed-sensitive at this sample size; $N = 3$ seeds are insufficient for a Wilcoxon test (minimum $p = 0.25$), and we rely on bootstrap confidence intervals for inference.
27	
28	% TODO: 1.5B × 5-seed bootstrap CI pending (C2 configuration). Will provide tighter effect size estimates and enable rank stability analysis.
29	% TODO: PubMed cross-domain replication (C3 configuration) in progress.
30	
31	\paragraph{Cross-method consistency.}
32	Six of seven monitored signals maintain consistent direction of change under both QLoRA and full fine-tuning (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}): spectral entropy, stable rank, evaluation perplexity, token entropy, distinct-1, and self-BLEU all move in the same direction as contamination increases, regardless of training method.
33	The exception is gradient norm, which exhibits non-monotonic behavior under QLoRA but monotonic decrease under full fine-tuning (Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
34	
35	\paragraph{Supporting rank correlations.}
36	Spearman correlations provide directional confirmation: spectral entropy maintains high $|\rho|$ across generations.
37	However, $|\rho| = 1.0$ for stable rank across contamination fractions is trivially achieved with few contamination levels and should not be interpreted as strong evidence of monotonicity.
38	We therefore use Cohen's $d$ as the primary sensitivity measure throughout.
39	
40	
41	\subsection{Within-Generation Early Warning}
42	\label{sec:early_warning_results}
43	
44	Cross-generation tracking (Section~\ref{sec:correlation}) requires completing multiple generations to assess severity.
45	A more actionable capability is detecting collapse \emph{within} a single training run, enabling early stopping before degradation accumulates.
46	We evaluate this using the relative deviation $\Delta_s$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:deviation}) and AUROC for binary collapse detection at each checkpoint.
47	
48	\paragraph{Stable rank as early alarm.}
49	At 10\% training progress, stable rank achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ for distinguishing $\alpha = 1.0$ from $\alpha = 0$ (Figure~\ref{fig:roc_curves}; Table~\ref{tab:within_gen_roc}).
50	At step~50 (3\% of training), $\Delta_\mathrm{SR} = 34.4\%$---far exceeding the 5\% detection threshold defined in Section~\ref{sec:evaluation}.
51	Stable rank's within-generation sensitivity reflects the structural nature of spectral signals: contamination alters the covariance eigenspectrum before output-level metrics respond, because energy dispersal across representation dimensions (Eq.~\ref{eq:stable_rank}) is detectable even when the model's token-level predictions have not yet degraded.
52	
53	% TODO: Figure~\ref{fig:roc_curves} uses synthetic ROC curves generated from known AUC values. Replace with empirical ROC data from plan001 5-seed validation.
54	
55	\paragraph{Spectral and token entropy.}
56	Token entropy deviates 24.5\% at step~50 but achieves only $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.478$ at 10\% progress---near random chance, indicating that despite its large initial deviation, token entropy provides no reliable within-generation discrimination.
57	Spectral entropy achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.773$, providing moderate within-generation discrimination but still substantially below stable rank's 0.984.
58	
59	\paragraph{Train loss: effective but interpretively ambiguous.}
60	Train loss achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$, the second-strongest within-generation signal after stable rank.
61	Its effectiveness has a straightforward explanation: contaminated training data has lower entropy than real text, so the model achieves lower loss when the training corpus contains synthetic data.
62	However, a decrease in train loss does not indicate improved model quality---it reflects successful fitting of corrupted data.
63	Practitioners should therefore cross-validate train loss signals with spectral indicators to distinguish benign from pathological loss reduction.
64	
65	\paragraph{Evaluation perplexity: no within-generation signal.}
66	Held-out evaluation perplexity achieves $\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$ at 10\% training progress---indistinguishable from random.
67	This failure has a structural explanation: within a single generation, the model trains on a fixed corpus $\mathcal{D}_g$ and has not yet produced the synthetic data that will contaminate $\mathcal{D}_{g+1}$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:mixing}).
68	Evaluation perplexity accumulates degradation \emph{across} generations through the iterative contamination loop, not within a single run.
69	Consequently, it does not diverge from baseline within one generation, even when collapse is underway.
70	
71	Spectral signals, by contrast, detect structural change---the dispersal of representational energy across dimensions---independent of whether the model's held-out predictions have degraded.
72	This yields a three-level hierarchy of within-generation detection capability: stable rank ($\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$) detects changes in weight-space structure, train loss ($\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.923$) detects changes in training data distribution, and held-out evaluation perplexity ($\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.534$) measures generalization quality---which does not diverge within a single generation.
73	The weakest link in within-generation monitoring is precisely the metric practitioners most commonly check at evaluation checkpoints.
74	
75	% TODO: Within-gen ROC results are from N=1 seed at 0.5B (C1). Pending plan001 5-seed validation at 1.5B to determine AUC stability.
76	
77	
78	\subsection{Signal Taxonomy: Method-Dependent Operating Ranges}
79	\label{sec:taxonomy}
80	
81	The cross-method comparison (C1 vs C4; Table~\ref{tab:configs}) reveals that the same signal can be informative under one training method and misleading under another.
82	
83	\paragraph{Gradient norm: method-dependent.}
84	Under QLoRA, gradient norm changes non-monotonically with contamination: $+5.9\%$ at $\alpha = 0.5$ relative to baseline, then $-30.8\%$ at $\alpha = 1.0$.
85	Under full fine-tuning, gradient norm decreases monotonically ($-58.3\%$ at $\alpha = 1.0$), consistent with the model converging more easily to lower-entropy synthetic data.
86	The QLoRA non-monotonicity likely arises from the interaction between contaminated gradients and the rank-$r$ adapter subspace~\citep{hu2022lora}: at moderate contamination, gradients from mixed real/synthetic data create conflicting update directions within the low-rank projection, inflating the norm; at full contamination, the uniform synthetic signal reduces this conflict~\citep{dettmers2023qlora}.
87	This method-dependence means gradient norm cannot be used as a collapse indicator without knowing the training configuration (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}).
88	
89	\paragraph{Downstream and diversity metrics: insensitive at 0.5B.}
90	HellaSwag accuracy is insensitive under QLoRA ($-1.15\%$, $d < 1$).
91	Under full fine-tuning, the $\alpha = 0$ baseline is unavailable, preventing direct effect size computation; the available data show accuracy of $0.397$ ($\alpha = 0.5$) and $0.372$ ($\alpha = 1.0$), a $-6.3\%$ relative change between these two conditions.
92	
93	Distinct-1 is non-monotonic under QLoRA: $+17.7\%$ at $\alpha = 0.5$ but $-12.5\%$ at $\alpha = 1.0$, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.
94	Self-BLEU is insensitive to collapse even when evaluation perplexity increases $17\times$.
95	This disconnect arises because nucleus sampling ($p = 0.95$) draws from the distribution tail, producing superficially diverse surface forms even as the model's probability mass concentrates on fewer modes.
96	Token entropy captures this concentration (Section~\ref{sec:correlation}), but $n$-gram diversity metrics do not.
97	Monitoring pipelines that rely solely on output diversity metrics risk missing collapse entirely.
98	
99	\paragraph{Full fine-tuning collapse dynamics.}
100	Full fine-tuning produces sharper onset but lower terminal severity than QLoRA (Figure~\ref{fig:method_comparison}).
101	The generation~0 to generation~1 evaluation perplexity ratio is $15.3\times$ under full FT versus $8.7\times$ under QLoRA.
102	However, post-onset growth saturates rapidly: the generation~1 to generation~4 ratio is $1.11\times$ under full FT (with a non-monotonic dip at generation~2, where $\mathrm{PPL}_\text{eval} = 288.5 < 294.0$ at generation~1), compared to continued growth under QLoRA.
103	Terminal severity at generation~4 is lower: full FT reaches $\mathrm{PPL}_\text{eval} = 326.19$ versus QLoRA's $839.2$ ($0.39\times$).
104	This pattern---fast onset, early saturation---is consistent with full parameter access enabling rapid adaptation to synthetic data characteristics, followed by convergence to a lower-entropy attractor.
105	
106	We observe that terminal stable rank values are similar across methods: $\mathrm{SR} = 153.87$ (full FT) versus $155.64$ (QLoRA) at generation~4, $\alpha = 1.0$.
107	This comparison rests on $N = 1$ seed per method and cannot be distinguished from coincidence; we note it as an observation, not a finding.
108	
109	\paragraph{Recommended operating ranges.}
110	Based on the evidence above, we propose signal-to-task assignments (Table~\ref{tab:signal_taxonomy}):
111	\begin{itemize}[nosep]
112	    \item \textbf{Within-generation alarm:} stable rank ($\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ at 10\% training progress).
113	    \item \textbf{Cross-generation severity discrimination:} spectral entropy ($d = 33.48$, $\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$).
114	    \item \textbf{Cross-generation tracking:} evaluation perplexity (large absolute effect, interpretable scale).
115	    \item \textbf{Method-dependent:} gradient norm (monotonic under full FT only).
116	    \item \textbf{Non-monotonic:} distinct-1 ($+17.7\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.5$, $-12.5\%$ at $\alpha{=}1.0$ under QLoRA).
117	    \item \textbf{Insensitive at 0.5B scale:} HellaSwag accuracy ($d < 1$).
118	\end{itemize}
119	
120	\noindent
121	These assignments are derived from 0.5B models on WikiText-2 with $N \leq 3$ seeds.
122	The full FT comparison uses $N = 1$ seed.
123	Validation across larger models, additional domains, and larger seed counts is required before these operating ranges can be considered robust.
124	
```

used as a collapse indicator without knowing the training configuration (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}).\n\n\\paragraph{Downstream and diversity metrics: insensitive at 0.5B.}\nHellaSwag accuracy is insensitive under QLoRA ($-1.15\\%$, $d < 1$).\nUnder full fine-tuning, the $\\alpha = 0$ baseline is unavailable, preventing direct effect size computation; the available data show accuracy of $0.397$ ($\\alpha = 0.5$) and $0.372$ ($\\alpha = 1.0$), a $-6.3\\%$ relative change between these two conditions.\n\nDistinct-1 is non-monotonic under QLoRA: $+17.7\\%$ at $\\alpha = 0.5$ but $-12.5\\%$ at $\\alpha = 1.0$, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.\nSelf-BLEU is insensitive to collapse even when evaluation perplexity increases $17\\times$.\nThis disconnect arises because nucleus sampling ($p = 0.95$) draws from the distribution tail, producing superficially diverse surface forms even as the model's probability mass concentrates on fewer modes.\nToken entropy captures this concentration (Section~\\ref{sec:correlation}), but $n$-gram diversity metrics do not.\nMonitoring pipelines that rely solely on output diversity metrics risk missing collapse entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Full fine-tuning collapse dynamics.}\nFull fine-tuning produces sharper onset but lower terminal severity than QLoRA (Figure~\\ref{fig:method_comparison}).\nThe generation~0 to generation~1 evaluation perplexity ratio is $15.3\\times$ under full FT versus $8.7\\times$ under QLoRA.\nHowever, post-onset growth saturates rapidly: the generation~1 to generation~4 ratio is $1.11\\times$ under full FT (with a non-monotonic dip at generation~2, where $\\mathrm{PPL}_\\text{eval} = 288.5 < 294.0$ at generation~1), compared to continued growth under QLoRA.\nTerminal severity at generation~4 is lower: full FT reaches $\\mathrm{PPL}_\\text{eval} = 326.19$ versus QLoRA's $839.2$ ($0.39\\times$).\nThis pattern---fast onset, early saturation---is consistent with full parameter access enabling rapid adaptation to synthetic data characteristics, followed by convergence to a lower-entropy attractor.\n\nWe observe that terminal stable rank values are similar across methods: $\\mathrm{SR} = 153.87$ (full FT) versus $155.64$ (QLoRA) at generation~4, $\\alpha = 1.0$.\nThis comparison rests on $N = 1$ seed per method and cannot be distinguished from coincidence; we note it as an observation, not a finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Recommended operating ranges.}\nBased on the evidence above, we propose signal-to-task assignments (Table~\\ref{tab:signal_taxonomy}):\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep]\n    \\item \\textbf{Within-generation alarm:} stable rank ($\\mathrm{AUROC} = 0.984$ at 10\\% training progress).\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-generation severity discrimination:} spectral entropy ($d = 33.48$, $\\mathrm{CV} = 0.0015$).\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-generation tracking:} evaluation perplexity (large absolute effect, interpretable scale).\n    \\item \\textbf{Method-dependent:} gradient norm (monotonic under full FT only).\n    \\item \\textbf{Non-monotonic:} distinct-1 ($+17.7\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.5$, $-12.5\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ under QLoRA).\n    \\item \\textbf{Insensitive at 0.5B scale:} HellaSwag accuracy ($d < 1$).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent\nThese assignments are derived from 0.5B models on WikiText-2 with $N \\leq 3$ seeds.\nThe full FT comparison uses $N = 1$ seed.\nValidation across larger models, additional domains, and larger seed counts is required before these operating ranges can be considered robust.\n",

    }

    "newString": "Cross-generation effect sizes are computed from $N = 3$ seeds (C2 configuration, interim 3 of 5 planned seeds); within-generation AUROC results use $N = 1$ seed at 0.5B (C1).",
    "originalFile": "% Limitations section — collapse_severity_monitor\n\nOur study has several limitations that bound the scope of our conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale.}\nOur primary experiments use Qwen2.5-0.5B and 1.5B models, with only a single-seed spot-check at 3B.\nWhether signal--severity relationships and operating ranges transfer to models at scales of practical deployment ($\\geq$7B) remains unvalidated.\nStable rank baselines are known to be scale-dependent~\\citep{Jha2025}, and threshold calibration may require per-scale adjustment.\n\n\\paragraph{Seed count and statistical power.}\nCross-generation effect sizes are computed from $N = 3$ seeds (C1 configuration); within-generation AUROC results use $N = 1$ seed at 0.5B.\nThe full fine-tuning comparison (C4) uses $N = 1$ seed.\nThese sample sizes are insufficient for rank-based tests (minimum $p = 0.25$ for Wilcoxon with $N = 3$), and we rely on bootstrap confidence intervals.\nStable rank's coefficient of variation ($\\mathrm{CV} = 0.022$) is an order of magnitude larger than spectral entropy's ($0.0015$), and its effect size dropped from $d = 61.7$ to $d = 9.43$ when a third seed was added---underscoring that our ranking of signals by sensitivity may shift with more seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain coverage.}\nWe evaluate on WikiText-2 and PubMed abstracts.\nWhether our signal taxonomy generalizes to code, dialogue, multilingual text, or instruction-following data is unknown.\n\n\\paragraph{Collapse source.}\nOur iterative retraining protocol simulates a specific collapse mechanism---self-consuming loops with controlled contamination fractions.\nReal-world collapse may involve mixed provenance (multiple model generations), distribution shift between generations, or partial synthetic contamination at unknown fractions.\nSignal behavior under these conditions may differ from our controlled setting.\n\n\\paragraph{Fine-tuning methods.}\nWe compare QLoRA and full fine-tuning.\nOther parameter-efficient methods (LoRA without quantization, adapters, prefix tuning) may interact differently with spectral signals due to different parameterization constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Causal claims.}\nWe establish correlations between signals and collapse severity, not causal mechanisms.\nThe spectral entropy increase under collapse reflects a change in representation structure, but we do not prove that this change causes (rather than accompanies) output degradation.\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch 追加 — RR1-C3 升级] Reviewer 完成 distinct-1 溯源，情况比预想严重：

**§4.3 line 113 的 "d < 1" 是伪统计符号**：
- 0.5B 每个方法 N=1 seed，组内方差不存在，Cohen's d 在数学上不可计算
- `signal_heatmap.py` 计算的是百分比变化（-2.76%），不是 Cohen's d
- **修正**：删除 `$d < 1$`，改为描述性语言："relative change < 3% under Full FT at 0.5B (N=1, no effect size computable)"

**§4.1 line 20 的 d=14.60 需要标注限制**：
- 数据确实来自 1.5B/plan006，但 seed44 的 distinct_1 为 null → effective N=2 非 3
- N=2 时 Cohen's d 方差估计极不稳定（stable_rank d=61.7→9.43 就是前车之鉴）
- **修正**：标注 "distinct-1 (d = 14.60, 1.5B, effective N = 2 seeds)"

**全局一致性**：
- distinct-1 不能同时出现在 Tier 1 列表（§4.1）和 "Insensitive" 列表（§4.3）
- 如果保留 d=14.60 作为 1.5B Tier 1 证据，§4.3 必须明确说 "insensitive **at 0.5B scale**" 并标注是 percentage change
- Main Agent 数据验证已不需要——Reviewer 溯源完成，直接按上述修正

_source line 1613 · 2026-05-15T23:55:20.533Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01P5aBWfnv3FTkMXTtCjLs3G`</summary>

```text
85	\paragraph{Downstream and diversity metrics: insensitive at 0.5B.}
86	HellaSwag accuracy is insensitive under QLoRA ($-1.15\%$, $d < 1$).
87	Under full fine-tuning, the $\alpha = 0$ baseline is unavailable, preventing direct effect size computation; the available data show accuracy of $0.397$ ($\alpha = 0.5$) and $0.372$ ($\alpha = 1.0$), a $-6.3\%$ relative change between these two conditions.
88	
89	Distinct-1 is non-monotonic under QLoRA: $+17.7\%$ at $\alpha = 0.5$ but $-12.5\%$ at $\alpha = 1.0$, reversing direction between moderate and full contamination.
90	Self-BLEU is insensitive to collapse even when evaluation perplexity increases $17\times$.
91	This disconnect arises because nucleus sampling ($p = 0.95$) draws from the distribution tail, producing superficially diverse surface forms even as the model's probability mass concentrates on fewer modes.
92	Token entropy captures this concentration (Section~\ref{sec:correlation}), but $n$-gram diversity metrics do not.
93	Monitoring pipelines that rely solely on output diversity metrics risk missing collapse entirely.
94	
95	\paragraph{Full fine-tuning collapse dynamics.}
96	Full fine-tuning produces sharper onset but lower terminal severity than QLoRA (Figure~\ref{fig:method_comparison}).
97	The generation~0 to generation~1 evaluation perplexity ratio is $15.3\times$ under full FT versus $8.7\times$ under QLoRA.
98	However, post-onset growth saturates rapidly: the generation~1 to generation~4 ratio is $1.11\times$ under full FT (with a non-monotonic dip at generation~2, where $\mathrm{PPL}_\text{eval} = 288.5 < 294.0$ at generation~1), compared to continued growth under QLoRA.
99	Terminal severity at generation~4 is lower: full FT reaches $\mathrm{PPL}_\text{eval} = 326.19$ versus QLoRA's $839.2$ ($0.39\times$).
100	This pattern---fast onset, early saturation---is consistent with full parameter access enabling rapid adaptation to synthetic data characteristics, followed by convergence to a lower-entropy attractor.
101	
102	We observe that terminal stable rank values are similar across methods: $\mathrm{SR} = 153.87$ (full FT) versus $155.64$ (QLoRA) at generation~4, $\alpha = 1.0$.
103	This comparison rests on $N = 1$ seed per method and cannot be distinguished from coincidence; we note it as an observation, not a finding.
104	
```

}
